Optimal vintage factor analysis with deflation varimax
Xin Bing, Xin He, Dian Jin, Yuqian Zhang

Abstract
Vintage factor analysis is one important type of factor analysis that aims to first find a low-dimensional representation of the original data, and then to seek a rotation such that the rotated low-dimensional representation is scientifically meaningful. Perhaps the most widely used vintage factor analysis is the Principal Component Analysis (PCA) followed by the varimax rotation. Despite its popularity, little theoretical guarantee can be provided mainly because varimax rotation requires to solve a non-convex optimization over the set of orthogonal matrices. In this paper, we propose a deflation varimax procedure that solves each row of an orthogonal matrix sequentially. In addition to its net computational gain and flexibility, we are able to fully establish theoretical guarantees for the proposed procedure in a broad context. Adopting this new varimax approach as the second step after PCA, we further analyze this two step procedure under a general class of factor models. Our results show that it estimates the factor loading matrix in the optimal rate when the signal-to-noise-ratio (SNR) is moderate or large. In the low SNR regime, we offer possible improvement over using PCA and the deflation procedure when the additive noise under the factor model is structured. The modified procedure is shown to be optimal in all SNR regimes. Our theory is valid for finite sample and allows the number of the latent factors to grow with the sample size as well as the ambient dimension to grow with, or even exceed, the sample size. Extensive simulation and real data analysis further corroborate our theoretical findings.



Keywords:
Factor analysis, varimax rotation, minimax optimal estimation, non-convex optimization, Independent Component Analysis, sparse dictionary learning.

1
Introduction

Vintage factor analysis is one important type of classical factor analysis, first developed by
[
61
,
62
]
and coined by
[
38
]
. It aims to first find a low-dimensional representation of the original data, and then to find a rotation such that the rotated low-dimensional representation is “scientifically meaningful”
[
61
]
. One widely used vintage factor analysis is the Principal Component Analysis (PCA) followed by the varimax rotation, first proposed by
[
38
]
. In this paper, we propose a variant of such two-step approach and analyze it under the following latent factor model

X=\mathbf{\Lambda}Z+E

(1.1)

where
X\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is an observable
p
-dimensional random vector,
Z\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
is an unobserved, latent factor with some
r\leq p
and
E\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is an additive error that is independent of
Z
. The loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}
is treated as deterministic and is the main quantity of our interest.

Suppose the observed
p\times n
data matrix
\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
consists of
n
i.i.d. samples of
X
according to (
1.1
). The first
r
Principal Components (PCs) of
\mathbf{X}
obtained from PCA are used as the low-dimensional representation of
\mathbf{Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
. Collect these PCs in the
r\times n
matrix
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
subjecting to
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\mathbf{U}_{(r)}^{\top}=n~\mathbf{I}_{r}
. Varimax rotation then seeks an
r\times r
orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{Q}
by solving the following optimization problem
1
1

1

The second term in the parenthesis does not affect the maximizer due to orthonormal rows of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
.

\begin{split}&\argmax_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}}~\sum_{k=1}^{r}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left([\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{U}_{(r)}]_{ki}^{4}-\left({1\over n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{U}_{(r)}]_{ki}^{2}\right)^{2}\right)\end{split}

(1.2)

We use the notion
\mathbb{O}_{p\times r}
to denote the set of
p\times r
matrices with orthonormal columns.
Despite being used widely, there has been long-standing controversy on the meaning and interpretation of varimax rotation. Recently,
[
53
]
shows that the global solution to the varimax criterion in (
1.2
) indeed makes statistical inference when the factor
Z
contains independent components, and each of which has a leptokurtic distribution, an assumption we will also adopt in this paper (see,
1
of
Section

3
for details).
However, optimizing over the Stiefel manifold
\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
renders (
1.2
) difficult to solve computationally. Furthermore, (
1.2
) is highly non-convex and its optimization landscape is notoriously difficult to analyze due to existence of various local solutions and spurious stationary points. Consequently, little theoretical guarantee to date can be made for any of the existing algorithms to solve (
1.2
).

In this paper, we propose a new deflation varimax approach that computes each row of an orthogonal matrix in a sequential manner by solving a variant of (
1.2
). Comparing to solving an
r\times r
orthogonal matrix simultaneously from (
1.2
), computing each row one at a time is in general more computationally efficient, and is also more flexible in practice as one can stop after obtaining certain number of rows. In addition to its net computational gain and flexibility, we are able to provide complete theoretical guarantees on the obtained rotation matrix in a broader context. Under model (
1.1
), we further adopt this deflation varimax procedure together with PCA to estimate the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
. Our theory shows that this approach estimates the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
optimally in the minimax sense.

1.1
Related literature

We begin by discussing related literature.

Factor analysis and factor rotation.

Model (
1.1
) has been extensively studied in the literature of classical factor analysis where the primary interest is typically on estimation of the column space of the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
, rather than
\mathbf{\Lambda}
itself. See, for instance,
[
31
,
43
,
5
]
. Although there exists various identification conditions on
\mathbf{\Lambda}
and the covariance matrices of
Z
and
E
under which
\mathbf{\Lambda}
becomes identifiable and can be consistently estimated
[
9
]
, most of them are “more or less arbitrary ways of determining the factor loadings uniquely. They do not correspond to any theoretical considerations of psychology; there is no inherent meaning on them.”
[
5
]
. For the same reason, a large body of the literature focus on recovering the latent space of the factor
Z
under model (
1.1
). PCA is arguably one of the most popular methods for predicting low-dimensional spaces, and a few leading PCs are typically used for predicting
\mathbf{Z}
up to orthogonal transformations of its rows. Analysis of the PCs for such purpose have been studied in existing literature
[
8
,
11
,
59
,
25
,
15
]
, just to name a few.

A different line of factor analysis that aims to identify and estimate the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is studied recently in
[
18
,
14
,
16
]
where
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is assumed to have multiple rows that are parallel to the canonical basis in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
and the noise
E
needs to have independent components. In this paper we do not resort to these conditions and instead require components of
Z
to be independent and leptokurtic. Under this line of assumption, a recent work
[
53
]
studies the rotated PCs by using varimax rotation under a different class of latent factor model. Their results reveal that performing varimax rotation eliminates the indeterminacy due to orthogonal transformations so that the rotated PCs consistently predict
\mathbf{Z}
only up to sign flip and permutation of its rows.
However, the results in
[
53
]
only hold for the global solution to (
1.2
).

Finding meaningful rotation in factor analysis has been a traditional topic, dating back to 1940s, see,
[
29
,
46
]
for detailed reviews and other references in the more recent work
[
53
]
. Orthogonal rotation due to its analytic tractability has been firstly studied and widely used since then. The two most popular examples are the quartimax rotation
[
47
]
(see, also its variant but equivalent criteria in
[
21
,
54
,
26
]
) and the (raw) varimax rotation in (
1.2
). Criteria of these orthogonal rotations are mathematically formulated to explicate Thurstone’s five formal rules for simple structure
[
62
]
. It is worth mentioning that when the rows of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
are orthonormal, both the quartimax rotation and the varimax rotation are equivalent. In
[
38
]
, a variant of the varimax rotation based on normalizing the
\ell_{2}
-norm of each column of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
in (
1.2
) is further advocated, but later in
[
40
]
the authors suggested to remove such normalization. On the other hand, computing the optimal orthogonal rotation over the Stiefel manifold has been a longstanding problem. In
[
38
,
39
]
a closed-form, necessary condition for solving a pair of orthonormal rows is derived and the author proposes to repeat such procedure for solving all distinct pairs of rows. This procedure becomes computationally expensive for moderate
r
and large
n
, and is also found to accumulate errors rapidly. Later in
[
30
]
and
[
56
]
fixed-point iterative algorithms are proposed to solve (
1.2
) for an orthogonal matrix simultaneously. Both algorithms are in vein similar to the projected gradient ascent algorithm which has been widely adopted for solving (
1.2
), and is still used to date in the
R
package
varimax
. Unfortunately, to the best of our knowledge, none of the existing algorithms has provable guarantee.

Independent Component Analysis (ICA) and complete dictionary learning.
ICA is one particular factor analysis that aims to extract independent, non-Gaussian signals
[
35
]
. A popular ICA model assumes that the observable feature
X
follows model (
1.1
) with
E=0
and
p=r
.
In the ICA literature, one of the most widely used algorithms for estimating
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is the FastICA which has two versions. The symmetric FastICA
2
2

2

FastICA has the flexibility of choosing different loss functions. In our context, we compare with the versions using kurtosis (the
\ell_{4}
-norm).

[
35
]
is in vein similar to solving the varimax criterion in (
1.2
) via a fixed-point algorithm while the deflation FastICA
[
34
]
is its sequential counterpart that aims to solve each row one at a time. Algorithmically,
[
34
]
shows that the symmetric FastICA converges to a stationary point in a cubic rate while
[
24
]
characterizes the stationary points of deflation FastICA at the population level when we have infinitely many samples. Although statistical properties of both versions of FastICA have been studied in various papers, for instance,
[
33
,
49
,
63
,
50
,
35
,
48
]
, these results are all established for the global solution instead of the actual ones computed by FastICA. Algorithms with both polynomial running time and provable guarantees are recently studied in
[
2
,
3
]
. Their procedure is based on tensor decomposition via power iterations. More recently,
[
7
]
establishes the minimax optimal rate of convergence, and the optimal sample complexity, of estimating
\mathbf{\Lambda}
under the ICA model. Moreover, inference of entries in
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is also studied in
[
7
]
.

Progress on understanding the optimization landscape and providing provable guarantees of solving (
1.2
) in a deflation manner has also been made in the past decade in a sub-model of ICA where entries of
Z
are further assumed to be i.i.d. from a Bernoulli-Gaussian distribution
[
57
,
60
,
52
,
75
,
77
,
36
]
. This sub-model finds its application in complete Dictionary Learning (DL), an important problem in the field of compressed sensing and signal recovery. In their language, columns of
\mathbf{\Lambda}
are called atoms and they form the dictionary which is the main quantity of interest. However, to the best of our knowledge, the existing results under this sub-model are only established for the noise-free case, namely,
E=0
(see also
Remark

5
for detailed comparison).

Although some of the aforementioned results in the ICA and DL literature could potentially be helpful for solving and analyzing the varimax rotation, the matrix
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
in (
1.2
) unfortunately has both an additive error and an approximation error in addition to the signal that we want to recover. What’s worse is that
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
also depends on an unknown rotation matrix which itself is random and dependent on the data
\mathbf{X}
(see details in
Theorem

1
). Dealing with such complication requires new procedures as well as new analysis. We defer to
Remark

5
of
Section

4.2
for more detailed technical comparison with the existing results of ICA and DL.

1.2
Our contributions

Our first contribution is to propose in
Section

2
a new approach,
deflation varimax rotation
, of computing an orthogonal rotation matrix row by row via solving the following variant of the varimax criterion in (
1.2
),

\displaystyle\min_{q\in\mathbb{S}^{r}}-\bigl\|\mathbf{U}_{(r)}^{\top}~q\bigr\|_{4}^{4}.

(1.3)

Here
\mathbb{S}^{r}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{r}:\|v\|_{2}=1\}
denotes the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
. To solve (
1.3
), we adopt the projected gradient descent (PGD) algorithm which is widely used for solving constrained optimization problems due to its simplicity and computational efficiency.
Specifically, for computing each row of the rotation matrix, we propose to solve the
same
optimization problem (
1.3
) via PGD but from
different
initializations. Choosing initialization is crucial due to the non-convexity of (
1.3
). In
Section

5
we offer and analyze two possible initialization schemes: one is based on random initialization, and the other one is based on method of moments estimation which is of interest on its own. After computing all rows, a symmetric orthogonalization is performed to ensure that the final rotation matrix is orthogonal. In
Section

2
, we further adopt the proposed deflation varimax rotation
in conjunction with PCA to estimate the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
under model (
1.1
). This new procedure is termed as
PCA with deflation varimax
.

To provide theoretical guarantees for PCA with deflation varimax, our second contribution is to establish a new decomposition for the matrix
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
of the PCs. We write the singular value decomposition (SVD) of the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}
with
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{O}_{p\times r}
and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
. We prove a new composition in
Theorem

1
of
Section

4
that

\mathbf{U}_{(r)}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Z}+\mathbf{N}+\mathbf{\Omega}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n},

(1.4)

where
\mathbf{R}
is some
r\times r
random orthogonal matrix,
\mathbf{N}
is an additive error with i.i.d. columns and
\mathbf{\Omega}
can be viewed as the approximation error of using PCA for estimating
\mathbf{L}
and
\mathbf{S}
(see, the paragraph after
Theorem

1
for detailed discussion). Our results in
Theorem

1
also state the explicit structure of the additive error
\mathbf{N}
, and quantify the approximation error
\mathbf{\Omega}
.
As mentioned before, the existing analysis of the PCs either assumes additional identifiability conditions or analyze
(\mathbf{U}_{(r)}-\mathbf{H}\mathbf{Z})
for an
r\times r
invertible matrix
\mathbf{H}
which is pre-specified mainly for mathematical convenience. Our new analysis of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
requires weaker conditions, and is more tailored for analyzing the subsequent varimax rotation. Indeed, the decomposition in (
1.4
) suggests the deflation varimax rotation applied to
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
estimate the orthogonal matrix
\bar{\mathbf{A}}:=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
.

Our third contribution is to provide a complete analysis of the proposed deflation varimax rotation in
Section

4
. In
Theorem

2
of
Section

4.2

we first analyze the optimization landscape of (
1.3
) by providing explicit characterization of its stationary points, in particular, the ones that are close to columns of
\bar{\mathbf{A}}
. Such result is generic, and is independent of
any
optimization algorithm one might deploy to solve (
1.3
).
The proximity of the stationary points, also known as the statistical error, in
Theorem

2
depends on the interplay of the approximation error
\mathbf{\Omega}
, the covariance matrix of columns in the additive error
\mathbf{N}
, the low-dimension
r
as well as the sample size
n
.
Particularizing to PGD, we show in
Theorem

3
that any stationary point from using PGD to solve (
1.3
) provably recovers one column of
\bar{\mathbf{A}}
provided that the initialization of PGD lies in the vicinity of that column. Our results also capture such requirement of initialization and are valid essentially for
any generic
initialization scheme. In
Section

5
we discuss and analyze two specific initialization schemes which are new for their own interests. In
Theorem

4
of
Section

4.2
, we further establish theoretical guarantees for the whole deflation varimax rotation obtained by solving (
1.3
)
r
times and followed by a symmetric orthogonalization. Our results show that the deflation varimax estimates
\bar{\mathbf{A}}
up to an
r\times r
signed permutation matrix and its estimation error in the Frobenius norm is shown to only increase
linearly in the dimension
r
comparing to that of estimating one column. Although our results in
Section

4.2
are mainly stated for the deflation varimax rotation applied to the PCs, our analysis are in fact carried out under a broader context where both
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{\Omega}
in (
1.4
) are allowed to be in general forms. As a result,
both our method and theory could be applied to other applications including both ICA and complete DL. See,
Remark

5
for details.

Our fourth contribution is to establish the minimax optimal rates of estimating the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
under model (
1.1
). In
Theorem

5
of
Section

4.3
, we
derive finite sample upper bounds of the estimation error in the Frobenius norm for the proposed estimator using PCA with deflation varimax.
To benchmark the upper bound, we further establish in
Theorem

6
minimax lower bounds of estimating
\mathbf{\Lambda}
under model (
1.1
). As a result, PCA with deflation varimax is minimax optimal (up to a logarithmic factor of
n
) when the signal-to-noise-ratio (SNR) in our problem is moderate or large.
In the weak signal regime, our theory reveals that the sub-optimality of PCA with deflation varimax is caused by both the additive error
\mathbf{N}
via PGD and the approximation error
\mathbf{\Omega}
via PCA. In
Section

6
we consider when the noise
E
under model (
1.1
) is structured and offer two steps of improvement over using PCA in the first step and deploying PGD to solve (
1.3
) in the second step. Specializing to the case when the covariance matrix of
E
is proportional to the identity matrix, we show in
Theorem

10
that the sub-optimality of PCA with deflation varimax could be remedied and
the improved estimator achieves the minimax optimal rate in all SNR regimes.

This paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
we state both the deflation varimax rotation for computing the rotation matrix and the proposed PCA with deflation varimax for estimating the loading matrix. In
Section

3
we state the main assumptions used in our analysis. Theoretical analysis of the decomposition of the PCs is presented in
Section

4.1
.
Section

4.2
contains a complete theory of analyzing the deflation varimax rotation. In
Section

4.3
we analyze the proposed estimator of the loading matrix. Initialization schemes are discussed in
Section

5
. In
Section

6
we discuss possible improvements when the noise
E
is structured.
Section

7
contains our real data analysis while simulation studies are presented in
Appendix

A
. All the proof is deferred to the appendix.

1.3
Notations

For any positive integer
d
, we write
[d]:=\{1,\ldots,d\}
.
For any vector
v
, we use
\|v\|_{q}
to denote its
\ell_{q}
norm for
0\leq q\leq\infty
.
We use uppercase bold fonts for matrices. For any
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times q}
, we use
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{M})\geq\sigma_{2}(\mathbf{M})\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{\min(d,q)}(\mathbf{M})
to denote its singular values. We define the operator norm
\|\mathbf{M}\|_{{\rm op}}=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{M})
and the Frobenius norm
\|\mathbf{M}\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}^{2}
. For any
i\in[d]
and
j\in[q]
, we use
M_{i\cdot}
and
M_{j}
to denote the
i
th row and the
j
th column, respectively.
For a symmetric positive semi-definite matrix
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
, we use
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{S})\geq\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{S})\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}(\mathbf{S})
to denote its eigenvalues.
For any two sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, we write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
if there exists some constant
C
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
.
The notation
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
stands for
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
b_{n}\lesssim a_{n}
. We use
\mathbf{I}_{d}
to denote the
d\times d
identity matrix and use
1
(
0
) to denote the vector with all ones (zeroes) whose dimension depends on the context. For
d_{1}\geq d_{2}
, we use
\mathbb{O}_{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
to denote the set of all
d_{1}\times d_{2}
matrices with orthonormal columns. We use
\mathbb{S}^{d}
to denote the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Lastly, we use
c,c^{\prime},C,C^{\prime}
to denote positive absolute constants that unless otherwise indicated may vary from line to line.

2
Methodology

In this section, we state our procedure of estimating the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
for a given low-dimension
r
. Selection of
r
is an important problem and we defer to
Section

7.1
for detailed discussion. As mentioned in the Introduction, we first apply PCA to the
p\times n
data matrix
\mathbf{X}
. Specifically, write the eigen-decomposition

{1\over n}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}v_{j}v_{j}^{\top}+\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}d_{j}v_{j}v_{j}^{\top}

(2.1)

with
\mathbf{D}_{(r)}=\diag(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
containing the largest
r
non-zero eigenvalues in non-increasing order, and the corresponding eigenvectors
\mathbf{V}_{(r)}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})
. The first
r
principal components of
\mathbf{X}
are

\mathbf{U}_{(r)}:=\mathbf{D}_{(r)}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}_{(r)}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}.

(2.2)

Due to
\mathbf{V}_{(r)}\in\mathbb{O}_{p\times r}
, we have
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\mathbf{U}_{(r)}^{\top}=n\mathbf{I}_{r}
.

2.1
Deflation varimax rotation

The second step after PCA is to compute an
r\times r
orthogonal matrix based on the first
r
PCs.
Instead of solving the varimax criterion in (
1.2
), we propose to compute its rows one at a time by solving the following optimization problem

\displaystyle\min_{q\in\mathbb{S}^{r}}~F\left(q;\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\right),\qquad\text{with}\quad F(q;\mathbf{U}_{(r)})=-\frac{1}{12n}\bigl\|\mathbf{U}_{(r)}^{\top}q\bigr\|_{4}^{4}.

(2.3)

To solve (
2.3
), we adopt the projected gradient descent (PGD) algorithm due to its computational efficiency. We choose to use the Riemannian gradient over the classical gradient as the former is more suitable for solving constrained optimization problem over manifolds. Specifically, let
\grad F
be the Riemannian gradient of
F
over the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{r}
, defined as

\grad F\left(q;\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\right)=-{1\over 3n}\mathbf{P}_{q}^{\perp}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left(q^{\top}U_{t}\right)^{3}U_{t}.

(2.4)

Such basic definition is reviewed in
Section

B.2
. Here we write
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{n})
. Let
P_{\mathbb{S}^{r}}
be the projection of any
r
-dimensional vector to
\mathbb{S}^{r}
.
For each
k\in[r]
, we solve the
k
th row by iterating

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(\ell+1)}=P_{\mathbb{S}^{r}}\left(\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(\ell)}-\gamma\grad F\bigl(\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(\ell)};\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\bigr)\right),\quad\text{for }\ \ell=0,1,2,\ldots

(2.5)

until convergence.
Here
\gamma>0
is some step size and we defer its choice to
Theorem

3
and
Remark

2
.
The only difference across
k\in[r]
lies in choosing the initializations in (
2.5
) for
\ell=0
. In
Section

5
we offer two initialization schemes.

After computing
\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}=(\widehat{Q}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{Q}_{r})
from (
2.5
), we further orthogonalize it by solving

\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}=\argmin_{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}}\|\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}-\mathbf{Q}\|_{F}.

(2.6)

Problem (
2.6
) can be computed simply via the singular value decomposition of
\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Finally, the rotation matrix
\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}
can be used to estimate the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
as

\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}={\mathbf{V}_{(r)}\mathbf{D}_{(r)}^{1/2}\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}\over\|\mathbf{V}_{(r)}\mathbf{D}_{(r)}^{1/2}\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}\|_{\rm op}}.

(2.7)

The normalization is due to the scaling constraint
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{\Lambda})=1
in
2
of
Section

3
.
For the reader’s convenience, we summarize in
Algorithm

1
the deflation varimax rotation as well as in
Algorithm

2
the PCA with deflation varimax.

Remark 1

(Comparison with the classical deflation procedure)
.

The commonly used strategy of sequentially recovering orthonormal vectors is to consider the constrained optimization problem based on previously recovered solutions. In our context, for computing
\widehat{Q}_{k}
with
2\leq k\leq r
, this corresponds to solving (
2.3
) over
q\in\mathbb{S}^{r}
subject to the constraint
q^{\top}\widehat{Q}_{i}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
.
Although this is a natural deflation procedure that produces an orthonormal
\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}
, it oftentimes has unstable practical performance as the resulting estimator could suffer from exploding error that accumulates exponentially as
r
increases
[
6
,
67
]
(see, also
[
50
,
6
,
70
]
in other similar problems). In our context, we find that such sequentially
constrained
optimization problems are not needed. Instead, the same
unconstrained
optimization coupled with proper initializations suffices to guarantee optimal estimation (see, Theorems
4
,
5
and
10
).

Data:

A matrix
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
and a positive integer
1\leq s\leq\min\{r,n\}
.

Result:

A matrix
\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times s}
.

Set
\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{0}
;

for

k=\{1,2,\ldots,s\}

do

Obtain any stationary point
\widehat{Q}_{k}
from solving
by (
2.5
);

end for

\textrm{SVD}(\widehat{\mathbf{Q}})=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Sigma}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
with
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times s}
and
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{O}_{s\times s}
;

Compute
\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
.

Algorithm 1

Deflation varimax rotation

Data:

A matrix
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
and a positive integer
1\leq r\leq\min\{p,n\}
.

Result:

A matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}
with
\sigma_{1}(\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}})=1
.

Compute
\mathbf{V}_{(r)}
and
\mathbf{D}_{(r)}^{1/2}
from (
2.1
);

Compute
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
from (
2.2
);

Apply
Algorithm

1
to
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
with
s=r
to obtain
\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
;

Compute
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}
from (
2.7
).

Algorithm 2

Principal component analysis with deflation varimax

3
Assumptions

We state the main assumptions that will be used in our analysis. Notice from model (
1.1
) that the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
cannot be identified even in the noiseless case
E=0
. This can be seen by inserting any
r\times r
invertible matrix
\mathbf{H}
such that

\widetilde{\mathbf{\Lambda}}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{H}
and
\widetilde{Z}=\mathbf{H}^{-1}Z
form the same model. We resort to the following assumptions on
\mathbf{\Lambda}
and
Z
.

Assumption 1
.

Assume that
Z\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
from model (
1.1
) has independent entries with zero mean, the second moment
\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}^{2}]=\sigma^{2}>0
and the excess kurtosis

\kappa:={1\over 3}\left({\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}^{4}]\over\sigma^{4}}-3\right)>0

for all
j\in[r]
.
Further assume each
Z_{j}/\sigma
is sub-Gaussian
3
3

3

A random variable
W
is said to be sub-Gaussian with sub-Gaussian constant
\gamma
if for any
t\geq 0
,
\mathbb{E}[\exp(tW)]\leq\exp(t^{2}\gamma^{2}/2)
.
with some sub-Gaussian constant
\gamma_{z}<\infty
.

There are two key ingredients in
1
: (1) entries of
Z
are independent, and (2) each entry
Z_{j}
has strictly positive excess kurtosis, that is, has
leptokurtic
distribution. Similar assumption has been adopted in
[
53
]
to study the statistical inference of varimax rotation. As commented therein, a sufficient condition for
Z_{j}
being leptokurtic is that
\mathbb{P}\{Z_{j}=0\}>5/6
, namely, realizations of
Z_{j}
could be exactly sparse (see, also Appendix G.2 of
[
53
]
, for discussion when entries of
Z
are approximately sparse). One such instance that is widely used in Dictionary Learning for modelling the sparsity of
Z_{j}
is the Bernoulli-Gaussian distribution
[
57
,
60
,
52
,
75
,
77
,
72
]
.
1
is also commonly used in the literature of Independent Component Analysis
[
35
]
since the excess kurtosis can be used to distinguish Gaussian from non-Gaussian distribution
[
28
]
. Meanwhile we remark that
Z_{j}
is not required to be identically distributed and the same second moment of
Z_{j}
can be assumed without loss of generality. Assuming the same excess kurtosis across
Z_{j}
is also made for simplicity, and under which the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
can only be identified up to a signed permutation (see,
Theorem

5
). In fact, if components of
Z
have distinct kurtoses, one can achieve stronger identifiability of
\mathbf{\Lambda}
by identifying the permutation as well. Finally, the sub-Gaussian tail of
Z_{j}
is not essential and can be replaced by sub-exponential tail or some moment conditions.

Assumption 2
.

Assume the
p\times r
loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
has rank
r
with
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{\Lambda})=1
and
\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{\Lambda})\geq c_{\Lambda}
for some absolute constant
c_{\Lambda}\in(0,1]
.

Assuming
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{\Lambda})=1
fixes the scale invariance between
\mathbf{\Lambda}
and
Z
, and can be made without loss of generality. The lower bound for
\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{\Lambda})
is made for more transparent presentation and one can track its dependence throughout our analysis.

Since our model (
1.1
) also contains the additive noise
E
, we need a condition on its distribution to separate it away from the signal
\mathbf{\Lambda}Z
.

Assumption 3
.

With
\sigma^{2}
defined in
1
, assume
E\sim\mathcal{N}_{p}(0,\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E})
for some
\epsilon^{2}\geq 0
and a semi-positive definite
p\times p
matrix
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}

with
\|\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}\|_{\rm op}=1
.

The Gaussianity in
3
is only made for theoretical convenience and can be relaxed to any centered, sub-Gaussian distribution in most of our results, see also
Remark

11
.

We allow correlated entries in
E
by considering a general covariance matrix
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}
. When
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
, the principal component analysis (PCA) yields the maximum likelihood estimator of
\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Z}
(treating
\mathbf{Z}
as deterministic), see, for instance,
[
43
,
9
]
. However, even in this case, it is well-known that PCA is consistent only if the noise
\mathbf{E}=(E_{1},\ldots,E_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
is dominated by the signal
\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Z}
in the sense that

{\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{E}\mathbf{E}^{\top})\over\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top})}\to 0,\quad\text{in probability as }n\to\infty.

In our setting at the population level, this gets translated to

{\epsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}\|_{\rm op}\over\sigma_{r}^{2}(\mathbf{\Lambda})}\leq{\epsilon^{2}\over c_{\Lambda}^{2}}\to 0,\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(3.1)

Our theory in
Section

4
shows that (
3.1
) ensures the validity of using PCA for estimating
\mathbf{\Lambda}
not only for
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
but also for a general
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}
. In
Section

6
we offer further improvement over using PCA in the first step when
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}
can be consistently estimated. In such case, the requirement (
3.1
) can be relaxed.

4
Theoretic guarantees

We first state in
Section

4.1
a decomposition of the principal components, which is informative for analyzing the subsequent deflation varimax rotation. In
Section

4.2
we provide analysis of the proposed deflation varimax rotation (
Algorithm

1
). We state theoretical guarantees on the proposed estimator of
\mathbf{\Lambda}
in
Section

4.3
.

4.1
Theoretical guarantees on the principal components

In this section we analyze the principal components
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
given by (
2.2
). For future reference, write the singular value decomposition (SVD) of the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
as

\mathbf{\Lambda}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}

(4.1)

with
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{O}_{p\times r}
,
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
and
\mathbf{S}=\diag(\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{\Lambda}),\ldots,\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{\Lambda}))\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
containing the singular values in non-increasing order. Define the deterministic sequence

\omega_{n}:=\sqrt{r\log(n)\over n}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}p\log(n)\over n}.

(4.2)

The following theorem states a decomposition of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
.

Theorem 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, assume there exists some sufficiently small constant
c>0
such that
\epsilon^{2}\leq c
,
r\log(n)\leq cn
and
\epsilon^{2}p\log(n)\leq cn
. Then there exists some
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
such that, with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
, one has

\mathbf{U}_{(r)}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Z}/\sigma+\mathbf{N}+\mathbf{\Omega}\right)

(4.3)

where
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{\top}\mathbf{E}/\sigma
and
\mathbf{\Omega}=\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathbf{Z}/\sigma+\mathbf{N})
with

\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}\lesssim\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2}.

Proof.

Its proof appears in
Section

C.1
.
∎

Theorem

1
ensures that

\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
approximates
\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Z}/\sigma
,
up to an additive error
\mathbf{N}
and an approximation error
\mathbf{\Omega}
.
Condition

r\log(n)\ll n
requires that the number of latent factors is much smaller than the sample size. Condition
\epsilon^{2}p\log(n)\ll n
holds in the low-dimensional case when
p\ll n
. In the high-dimensional case, it is commonly assumed in the existing literature of factor analysis
[
59
,
9
,
41
,
25
,
15
]
that
\epsilon^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1/p)
whence
\epsilon^{2}p\log(n)/n=\mathcal{O}(\log(n)/n)
. For instance, if a fixed proportion of rows in
\mathbf{\Lambda}
are i.i.d. from a
r
-dimensional distribution with its covariance matrix having bounded eigenvalues, then the largest
r
eigenvalues of
\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}
are all of order
p
. In this case, after transferring this scale to
\mathbf{Z}
under
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{\Lambda})=1
and provided that
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}
has bounded operator norm, we have
\epsilon^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1/p)
.
Regardless, the condition on
\epsilon^{2}
we require in
Theorem

1
is much weaker.

In view of the decomposition in (
4.3
), both
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{\Omega}
affect the error of using
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
to approximate the span of
\mathbf{Z}
. Specifically, the term
\mathbf{N}
is the intrinsic bias of PCA (see, also, the discussion before display (
3.1
)), and is still additive due to the independence between
\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{E}
. On the other hand, the term
\mathbf{\Omega}
contains two sources of estimation error: the error of the eigenvectors
\mathbf{V}_{(r)}
for estimating
\mathbf{L}
and the error of the eigenvalues
\mathbf{D}_{(r)}
for estimating
\sigma^{2}\mathbf{S}^{2}
. In cases when these two errors are expected to be large, one should seek more appropriate estimators of
\mathbf{L}
and
\sigma^{2}\mathbf{S}^{2}
. For instance, in
Section

6
we consider an improved estimator of
\sigma^{2}\mathbf{S}^{2}
when
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}
can be consistently estimated. If sparsity of the column space of
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is a reasonable assumption, one could use the sparse PCA to estimate
\mathbf{L}
instead.
Nevertheless, our analysis of
Theorem

1
remains valid once the same type of error bounds of new estimators can be derived.

To investigate which quantity the subsequent deflation varimax rotation
estimates, one needs to analyze the optimization problem in (
2.3
). The decomposition in (
4.3
) provides a key starting point for this. In the next section, we show that the deflation varimax in fact estimates the matrix

\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
in (
4.3
) up to a signed permutation matrix.

4.2
Theoretical guarantees of the deflation varimax in
Algorithm

1

To provide complete analysis of the deflation varimax rotation
\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}
obtained in (
2.6
), we start by analyzing the solution to (
2.3
). To this end, we introduce the oracle counterpart of (
2.3
)

\min_{q\in\mathbb{S}^{r}}F\left(q;\mathbf{R}\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\right).

(4.4)

We call (
4.4
) oracle as
\mathbf{R}
is not known in advance. Nevertheless,
Lemma

1
below provides the connection between (
2.3
) and (
4.4
) in terms of their PGD iterates in (
2.5
).

Lemma 1
.

Let
\{\widehat{q}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell\geq 0}
and
\{\widetilde{q}^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell\geq 0}
be any iterates obtained via the projected gradient descent in (
2.5
) for solving (
2.3
) and (
4.4
), respectively. Then whenever

\widehat{q}^{(0)}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\widetilde{q}^{(0)}

holds, one has

\widehat{q}^{(\ell)}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\widetilde{q}^{(\ell)},\qquad\text{for all }\ell\geq 1.

Proof.

Its proof appears in
Section

B.3
.
∎

In view of
Lemma

1
, we only need to analyze the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}
obtained from solving (
4.4
)
r
times. This brings great convenience to our analysis as

\mathbf{R}\mathbf{U}_{(r)}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Z}/\sigma+\mathbf{N}+\mathbf{\Omega}

(4.5)

has an additive decomposition in its right hand side, as opposed to
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
in (
4.3
).

We proceed to analyze the solution obtained from solving (
4.4
) once (for
k=1
).
Specifically, we provide in the following theorem an optimization landscape analysis of its stationary points. Such results are generic and independent of any particular optimization algorithm one might use to solve (
4.4
).
For simplicity and also without loss of generality, we assume
\sigma^{2}=1
from now on. Denote by
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
the covariance matrix of columns in
\mathbf{N}
. For future reference, we note that under Assumptions
2
&
3
,

\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}=\epsilon^{2}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{\top}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}\mathbf{L}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}\|_{\rm op}\leq\epsilon^{2}/c_{\Lambda}^{2}.

(4.6)

Also define
\sigma_{N}^{2}:=\sup_{q\in\mathbb{S}^{r}}\|\mathbf{P}_{q}^{\perp}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}q\|_{2}
which quantifies the extent to which
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
departs from a matrix that is proportional to
\mathbf{I}_{r}
. Recall from
Theorem

1
that
\mathbf{\Omega}=\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathbf{Z}+\mathbf{N})
.

Theorem 2
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, assume
\kappa\geq c
for some constant
c>0
and

\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}+\sigma_{N}^{2}+\sqrt{r^{2}\log(n)\over n}~\leq~c^{\prime}

(4.7)

for some sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime}>0
.
Let
q
be any stationary point to (
4.4
) such that

(A_{i}^{\top}q)^{2}\geq 1/3,~\quad|A_{i}^{\top}q|-\max_{j\neq i}|A_{j}^{\top}q|\geq C^{\prime}\left(\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}+\sigma_{N}^{2}+\sqrt{r^{2}\log(n)\over n}\right)

(4.8)

holds for some arbitrary
i\in[r]
and some positive constant
C^{\prime}
. Then with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
, one has

\min\Bigl\{\|q-A_{i}\|_{2},~\|q+A_{i}\|_{2}\Bigr\}~\lesssim~\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}+\sigma_{N}^{2}+\sqrt{r\log(n)\over n}.

(4.9)

Proof.

Its proof appears in
Section

B.4
.
∎

Surprisingly, despite the non-convexity of (
4.4
),
Theorem

2
shows that any stationary point
q
satisfying (
4.8
) consistently estimates one column of
\mathbf{A}
up to its sign. In particular,
any
first-order descent algorithm, including the projected gradient descent (PGD) in (
2.5
), can be used to estimate
\mathbf{A}
as long as the resulting stationary point satisfies (
4.8
).
The first condition in (
4.8
) requires
q
to align well with one column of
\mathbf{A}
while the second one puts a lower bound on the gap between the largest two entries (in absolute value) of
\mathbf{A}^{\top}q
. It is worth explaining the latter. Our proof of
Theorem

2
reveals that, in addition to the stationary points that are close to columns of
\mathbf{A}
, there exist several “bad” stationary points, say
\bar{q}\in\mathbb{S}^{r}
, which at the population level satisfy

\mathbf{A}^{\top}\bar{q}={1\over\sqrt{k}}\begin{bmatrix}1_{k}\\
0_{r-k}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{for any $2\leq k\leq r$,}

(4.10)

up to sign and permutation. The second condition in (
4.8
) excludes such bad stationary points.

A key step in our proof is to establish concentration inequalities between the objective function
F(q;\mathbf{R}\mathbf{U}_{(r)})
, its Riemannian gradient
\grad F(q;\mathbf{R}\mathbf{U}_{(r)})
and their population-level counterparts (see,
Lemmas
2
,
18
,
20
&
22
). In addition to
\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}
and
\sigma_{N}^{2}
, both terms
r\log(n)/n
and
r^{2}\log(n)/n
in displays (
4.7
) – (
4.9
) originate from these concentration inequalities, depending on whether the result needs to hold uniformly over
q\in\mathbb{S}^{r}
(see, displays (
B.18
), (
B.19
) and (
B.20
), for explicit expressions).
Condition
\kappa>c
is made for simplicity, and our analysis can be easily extended to allow
\kappa=\kappa(n)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Finally, the constant
1/3
in (
4.8
) is chosen for simplicity and we did not attempt to optimize such explicit constant.

Recall the decomposition of
\mathbf{R}\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
in (
4.3
).
Our results in
Theorem

2
characterize both the effect of the approximation error
\mathbf{\Omega}
and that of the additive error
\mathbf{N}
on estimating
\mathbf{A}
via solving (
4.4
). Specifically, the approximation error in
\mathbf{\Omega}
affects the estimation error in terms of
\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}
which according to
Theorem

1
is bounded from above by
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2})
. On the other hand, the additive error in
\mathbf{N}
affects the estimation error via the covariance matrix of its columns. When
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
is proportional to the identity matrix, one has
\sigma_{N}^{2}=0
, leading to a weaker condition in (
4.8
) as well as a faster rate in (
4.9
). For a general
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
given by (
4.6
), one always has
\sigma_{N}^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}/c_{\Lambda}^{2}
. We opt to state
Theorem

2
in its current form to reveal both effects of
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{\Omega}
as they are informative for understanding the improvements discussed later in
Section

6
. For now, we substitute
(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2})
for
(\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}+\sigma_{N}^{2})
to simplify the presentation.

To make use of
Theorem

2
for the PGD iteration in (
2.5
), we need to verify (
4.8
) for the corresponding stationary point. Due to non-convexity of (
4.4
), this naturally depends on the initialization in (
2.5
). The following theorem provides sufficient conditions under which
any generic
initialization provably ensures condition (
4.8
), hence recovers one column of
\mathbf{A}
. To formally state the result, let
\{q^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell\geq 0}
be a generic sequence of the PGD iterates in (
2.5
) from solving (
4.4
). We introduce the following event on the initial point
q^{(0)}
: for any
i\in[r]
and any numbers
0<\nu<\mu<1
,

\mathcal{E}^{(i)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)=\left\{|A_{i}^{\top}q^{(0)}|\geq\mu,~|A_{i}^{\top}q^{(0)}|-\max_{j\neq i}|A_{j}^{\top}q^{(0)}|\geq\nu\right\}.

(4.11)

Theorem 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, assume
\kappa\geq c
for some constant
c>0
. For any
i\in[r]
, assume
q^{(0)}
satisfies
\mathcal{E}^{(i)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
with some
0<\nu<\mu<1
such that

\sqrt{r^{2}\log(n)\over n}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}p\log(n)\over n}+\epsilon^{2}~\leq c^{\prime}\mu\nu^{2}

(4.12)

holds for some sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime}>0
. Then by choosing the step size
\gamma\leq c^{\prime\prime}/\kappa
for some small constant
c^{\prime\prime}>0
,
with probability at least
1-2/n
, any stationary point
q
of the PGD iterates
\{q^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell\geq 0}
to (
4.4
) satisfies

\min\Bigl\{\|q-A_{i}\|_{2},~\|q+A_{i}\|_{2}\Bigr\}~\lesssim~\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2}.

(4.13)

Moreover, with the same probability, (
4.13
) is guaranteed to hold for any
q^{(\ell)}
as long as
\ell\geq C\log(n/\max\{r,\epsilon^{2}p\})
.

The proof of
Theorem

3
appears in
Section

B.6
. The key to prove
Theorem

3
is to verify condition (
4.8
) for any obtained stationary point corresponding to the initialization
q^{(0)}
. To this end, in Lemmas
5
&
6
of
Section

B.5
, we prove that the event
\mathcal{E}^{(i)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
, condition (
4.12
) and
\gamma\leq c^{\prime\prime}/\kappa
ensure that, for any
\ell\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|A_{i}^{\top}q^{(\ell+1)}|-\max_{j\neq i}|A_{j}^{\top}q^{(\ell+1)}|\geq\min\bigl\{\nu,~1/4\bigr\}

(4.14)

as well as

\begin{split}&|A_{i}^{\top}q^{(\ell+1)}|\geq|A_{i}^{\top}q^{(\ell)}|\left(1+\gamma~\mu\nu\right),\hskip 85.35826pt\text{ if }\mu\leq|A_{i}^{\top}q^{(\ell)}|\leq\sqrt{3}/2;\\
&|A_{i}^{\top}q^{(\ell+\ell^{\prime})}|\geq 1-(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2})^{2}-\left(1-\gamma\right)^{\ell^{\prime}},~\forall~\ell^{\prime}\geq 1,\quad\text{ if }\sqrt{3}/2<|A_{i}^{\top}q^{(\ell)}|\leq 1.\end{split}

(4.15)

Consequently, displays (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) together with condition (
4.12
) ensure that any stationary point
q
satisfies (
4.8
), hence consistently estimates
A_{i}
up to its sign. Moreover, by using the identity
\|A_{i}-v\|_{2}^{2}=2(1-A_{i}^{\top}v)
for any
v\in\mathbb{S}^{r}
, it is easy to see that the contraction property in (
4.15
) guarantees (
4.13
) after
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
iterations.

Remark 2

(Choice of the step size)
.

To ensure both the local contraction in (
4.15
) and algorithmic convergence,

Theorem

3
requires the step size
\gamma
can not be chosen too large. On the other hand, although
a too small choice of
\gamma
will not affect the statistical accuracy ultimately, it slows down the speed of algorithmic convergence, as seen from (
4.15
). In our extensive simulation, we found that
\gamma=10^{-5}
yields overall satisfactory results.

Remark 3

(Effect of the initialization)
.

Thanks to
Theorem

2
, we are able to provide sufficient conditions in terms of
\mu
and
\nu
under which
any generic
initialization provably recovers one column of
\mathbf{A}
. Furthermore, condition (
4.12
) in
Theorem

3
reveals that a better initialization, in the sense of larger
\mu
and
\nu
, leads to a weaker requirement on both the sample size
n
and the noise level
\epsilon^{2}
.
In
Section

5
, we discuss two initialization schemes and establish their corresponding orders of
\mu
and
\nu
for which
\mathcal{E}^{(i)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
holds.

Following
Theorem

3
, we immediately have theoretical guarantees for the whole matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}=(\widetilde{Q}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{r})
obtained from solving (
4.4
)
r
times via PGD. A key observation for analyzing
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}
is that the solutions
\widetilde{Q}_{k}
, for each
k\in[r]
, are obtained via the same PGD iterates but different initializations. Therefore, results in (
4.14
) and (
4.15
)
as well as
Theorem

3
are still applicable to analyze each
\widetilde{Q}_{k}
. This is summarized in the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

Grant the conditions in
Theorem

3
.
Assume there exists some permutation
\pi:[r]\to[r]
such that the initialization
\widetilde{Q}_{\pi(k)}^{(0)}
satisfies
\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
for all
k\in[r]
. Then there exists some signed permutation matrix
\mathbf{P}
such that with probability at least
1-2/n
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{P}\|_{F}~\lesssim~(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2})\sqrt{r}.

Note that
\widetilde{Q}_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{r}^{(0)}
in
Corollary

1
correspond to the initial points of the oracle problem in (
4.4
) whereas in practice we only have access to initializations to (
2.3
). Nevertheless,
Lemma

1
ensures that we only need to establish the order of
\mu
and
\nu
such that
\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
holds for
\widetilde{Q}_{k}^{(0)}:=\mathbf{R}\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(0)}
for each
k\in[r]
, with
\widehat{Q}_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,\widehat{Q}_{r}^{(0)}
being any initialization of the PGD iteration to solve (
2.3
). The following theorem states the theoretical guarantees of the corresponding solution
\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}
obtained from applying
Algorithm

1
to (
2.3
).

Theorem 4
.

Grant the conditions in
Theorem

3
.
Assume there exists some permutation
\pi:[r]\to[r]
such that
\mathbf{R}\widehat{Q}_{\pi(k)}^{(0)}
satisfies
\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
for all
k\in[r]
. Then there exists some signed permutation matrix
\mathbf{P}
such that with probability at least
1-2/n
,

\displaystyle\|\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}-\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{P}\|_{F}~\lesssim~(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2})\sqrt{r}.

Proof.

Its proof appears in
Section

B.7
.
∎

Remark 4

(Rotated principal components from the deflation varimax)
.

On the one hand,
Theorem

4
shows that the proposed deflation varimax rotation makes valid statistical inference, namely, it consistently estimates
\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
up to a signed permutation matrix. Similar results are proved in
[
53
]
for the global solution to the varimax criterion in (
1.2
) under a different model. On the other hand, by recalling the decomposition in (
4.3
) of
Theorem

1
, we see that the rotated PCs of using the deflation varimax satisfy

\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\approx\widecheck{\mathbf{Q}}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Z}+\mathbf{N})\approx\mathbf{P}^{\top}(\mathbf{Z}+\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{N}).

In words, the rotated PCs predict the factor matrix
\mathbf{Z}
, up to a scaling (
\sigma=1
) and signed permutation of the rows as well as an additive error matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{N}
. Furthermore, by the structure of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
in (
4.6
), columns of
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{N}
are i.i.d. Gaussian under
3
with zero mean and the covariance matrix bounded by
\epsilon^{2}/c_{\Lambda}^{2}
in the operator norm.

Remark 5

(Analysis of the deflation varimax in a broader context)
.

Although theoretical guarantees of the proposed deflation varimax so far are stated for the PCs under the decomposition in (
4.3
), our analysis in fact is carried out under a more general setting. Concretely, suppose one has access to an
r\times n
matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}
that satisfies

\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Z}+\mathbf{N}+\mathbf{\Omega}\right)

(4.16)

where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
is a deterministic matrix while
\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{N}
have i.i.d. columns satisfying Assumptions
1
&
3
, respectively, with
\sigma^{2}=1
and some general covariance matrix
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
.
In (
4.16
) both the rotation matrix

\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
and the approximation matrix
\mathbf{\Omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
could be random, and are further allowed to correlate with each other, as well as with
\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{N}
. In
Appendix

B
we give detailed statements of our theoretical guarantees of the proposed deflation varimax under this general model. Clearly the decomposition of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
in (
4.3
) is one instance of model (
4.16
). Below we discuss another two important examples that are subcases of model (
4.16
), and to which both our procedure and our theory are applicable.

(a)

Independent Component Analysis (ICA).
ICA often assumes the model
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{Z}
for a deterministic, non-singular matrix
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
and a random matrix
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
with independent, non-Gaussian entries that have zero mean and equal variance
\sigma^{2}>0
. A common first step of various ICA approaches is to whiten the data by using the square root matrix of the sample covariance matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{Y}=n^{-1}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{Y}^{\top}

[
35
]
. Consequently, by writing
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{Y}=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{Y}]
and the SVD of
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{V}_{L}\mathbf{D}_{L}\mathbf{U}_{L}^{\top}
, we have

\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{Y}^{-1/2}\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{V}_{L}\mathbf{U}_{L}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}/\sigma+\bigl(\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{Y}^{-1/2}-\mathbf{\Sigma}_{Y}^{-1/2}\bigr)\mathbf{Y}.

This is a sub-model of (
4.16
) with
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
,
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{V}_{L}\mathbf{U}_{L}^{\top}
,
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{\Omega}=(\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{Y}^{-1/2}-\mathbf{\Sigma}_{Y}^{-1/2})\mathbf{Y}
. When entries of
\mathbf{Z}
have leptokurtic distributions, our proposed deflation varimax can be applied to estimate
\mathbf{V}_{L}\mathbf{U}_{L}^{\top}
and to further estimate the matrix
\mathbf{L}
. Moreover, our theory developed in
Section

4.2
provides provable guarantees for the resulting estimator.

We now compare with the existing provable ICA algorithms from a technical perspective. Under model
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{Z}
with an orthonormal
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
,
[
2
]
proposes an algorithm for estimating
\mathbf{L}
via orthogonal tensor decomposition and power iterations in a deflation manner. This estimator, together with the subsequent analysis in
[
4
]
, has the estimation error under the Frobenius norm of order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{r^{2}/n}+{r^{5/2}/n})
, up to some multiplicative polynomial factor of
\log(n)
, provided that
n\gg r^{5/2}
. In
[
7
]
, an improved estimator which is obtained via fixed-point iterations and also in a deflation manner is shown to achieve the minimax optimal rate
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{r^{2}/n})
under the Frobenius norm. The required sample complexity is also reduced to
n\gg r^{2}
. To estimate a general non-singular matrix
\mathbf{L}
,
[
3
]
shows that a refined yet complicated procedure based on tensor decomposition and clustering provably estimates
\mathbf{L}
in the same rate as
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{r^{2}/n}+{r^{5/2}/n})
under the Frobenius norm but with a slower column-wise
\ell_{2}
-norm. Differently,
[
7
]
adopts data splitting for whitening and proves that the same optimal rate
\sqrt{r^{2}/n}
can be achieved under the same sample complexity.

In our case of model (
4.16
), we have to deal with both the additive error
\mathbf{N}
and the approximation error
\mathbf{\Omega}
as well as the random rotation
\mathbf{R}
. Consequently both the estimation error and the required sample complexity consist of other terms in addition to
\sqrt{r^{2}/n}
. On the other hand, our analysis in
Theorem

3
is able to characterize the effect of
any generic
initialization whereas both the analysis in
[
3
,
4
]
and
[
7
]
is only valid for a sufficiently good initialization (
\mu
in (
4.11
) needs to be close to
1
whence
\nu
is also of constant order). Finally, our method does not rely on either data splitting or complicated tensor decomposition.

(b)

Complete Dictionary Learning.

As mentioned in Section
1
, another related problem is the complete dictionary learning where the input data
\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}
follows model (
4.16
) with
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
,
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{\Omega}=\mathbf{0}
and entries of
\mathbf{Z}
are further assumed to be i.i.d. from a Bernoulli-Gaussian distribution. In this setting, a line of recent works
[
57
,
60
,
12
,
52
,
75
,
74
,
36
]
focus on designing provable algorithms to recover
\mathbf{A}
. In particular,
[
60
,
12
,
77
,
36
]
also consider the optimization problem in (
2.3
) and analyze its optimization landscape.
However, our results developed in this section are applicable in much broader settings.

4.3
Theoretical guarantees on estimating the loading matrix

Armed with the theoretical guarantees of the deflation varimax rotation in Theorems
7
and
8
, we now establish finite sample upper bounds of the estimation error of
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}
given by (
2.7
) under the Frobenius norm.

Theorem 5
.

Grant conditions in
Theorem

4
as well as its conclusion. For the same signed permutation
\mathbf{P}
therein, the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}
in (
2.7
) satisfies

\|\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{P}\|_{F}~=~\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\omega_{n}\sqrt{r}+\epsilon^{2}\sqrt{r}\right).

(4.17)

Proof.

Its proof appears in
Section

C.2
.
∎

Theorem

5
also implies that the loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is identifiable up to an
r\times r
signed permutation matrix. As mentioned after
1
, if all the coordinates of
Z
in model (
1.1
) have distinct kurtoses, such permutation ambiguity can be removed.

Remark 6

(Effects of the ambient dimension and the signal-to-noise ratio)
.

We discuss how the ambient dimension
p
and the reciprocal of the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR)
\epsilon^{2}
affect the estimation of
\mathbf{\Lambda}
. After ignoring the
\log(n)
term, display (
4.17
) in high-dimensional case (
p\geq n
) reduces to

\|\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{P}\|_{F}~=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{r^{2}\over n}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}pr\over n}\right).

(4.18)

In the low-dimensional case with
n\epsilon^{2}\leq p<n
, we have the same rate as above.
Theorem

6
below shows that the rate in (
4.18
) is in fact minimax optimal under model (
1.1
). On the other hand, if
p<n\epsilon^{2}
, then the rate in (
4.17
) becomes

\|\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{P}\|_{F}~=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{r^{2}\over n}+\epsilon^{2}\sqrt{r}\right).

(4.19)

If additionally
r\leq n\epsilon^{4}
holds, the second term becomes dominant. Therefore, the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}
is sub-optimal when
the SNR is small in the precise sense that

\epsilon^{2}\geq\min\{p/n,\sqrt{r/n}\}
.
In
Section

6
we show that this sub-optimality can be remedied if the noise
E
is structured.

To benchmark the rate obtained in
Theorem

5
, we establish the minimax lower bounds of estimating
\mathbf{\Lambda}
in the following theorem. Consider the following parameter space of
\mathbf{\Lambda}
:

\mathcal{A}:=\left\{\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}:\mathbf{\Lambda}\text{ satisfies \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ass_A_general}}\right\}.

Our minimax lower bounds are established for a fixed distribution of
Z
under
1
and
E\sim\mathcal{N}_{p}(0,\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p})
. Let
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Lambda},\epsilon}
denote the distribution of
\mathbf{X}
parametrized by both
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\epsilon^{2}
, the reciprocal of the SNR.

Theorem 6
.

Under model (
1.1
) and Assumptions
1
&
3
with
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
, assume
r(p-1)\geq 50
.
Then there exists some absolute constants
c>0
and
c^{\prime}\in(0,1)
such that

\inf_{\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}}\sup_{\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathcal{A},\epsilon^{2}\geq 0}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Lambda},\epsilon}\left\{\|\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\|_{F}\geq c\sqrt{r^{2}\over n}+c\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}pr\over n}\right\}\geq c^{\prime}.

Here the infimum is taken over all estimators.

Proof.

The proof appears in
Section

C.3
.
∎

Theorem

6
implies that the rate in (
4.18
) is minimax optimal, and cannot be further improved.

Our lower bounds in
Theorem

6
are new in the literature. In the special case
\epsilon^{2}=0
whence
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Z}
,
[
7
]
proves that the minimax lower bounds of estimating
\mathbf{\Lambda}
are of order
\sqrt{r^{2}/n}
under Assumptions
1
&
2
. For
\epsilon^{2}>0
, minimax lower bounds of estimating
\mathbf{\Lambda}
when
\mathbf{\Lambda}
has orthonormal columns are established in
[
69
]
. Although
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{O}_{p\times r}
satisfies
2
, the results in
[
69
]
are proved under a class of Gaussian distributions of
X
under model (
1.1
), hence are not applicable to our setting under
1
. For non-Gaussian factor
Z
and some loading matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}
with columns having the unit
\ell_{2}
-norm,
[
37
]
establishes a local minimax lower bound of estimating
\mathbf{\Lambda}
in the context of ICA. Our proof makes adaptation of their results to our context.

5
Initialization

Since we adopt the same PGD in (
2.5
) multiple times, it is crucial to have proper initializations. Our analysis in
Theorem

3
of
Section

4
shows that the initial point needs to satisfy
\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
in (
4.11
).
We discuss below two possible initialization schemes that provably ensure such requirement.

5.1
Random Initialization

The first initialization scheme is to randomly initialize
\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(0)}
in (
2.5
) via standard normal entries. Specifically, for each
1\leq k<r
, by writing
\widehat{Q}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{Q}_{k}
as the previously obtained solutions, we define
\widehat{\mathbf{P}}_{0}^{\perp}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
and

\widehat{\mathbf{P}}_{k}^{\perp}=\mathbf{I}_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widehat{Q}_{i}\widehat{Q}_{i}^{\top}.

(5.1)

Let the matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{(-k)}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times(r-k)}
contain the leading
(r-k)
eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{P}}_{k}^{\perp}
. Further let
g_{(-k)}\in\mathbb{R}^{r-k}
contain i.i.d. entries of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. For solving (
2.3
) the
(k+1)
th time, we propose to intialize (
2.5
) by

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{k+1}^{(0)}=P_{\mathbb{S}^{r}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{(-k)}g_{(-k)}\bigr).

(5.2)

The following theorem establishes its corresponding order of
\mu
and
\nu
for which the event
\mathcal{E}^{(k+1)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
in (
4.11
) holds.

Theorem 7

(Random Initialization)
.

Grant Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
as well as
\kappa\geq c
for some constant
c>0
. Choose
\gamma\leq c^{\prime}/\kappa
for some sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime}>0
.
Fix any
\delta\in(0,1/r^{2})
and assume there exists some sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime\prime}>0
such that

r^{3/2}\left(\sqrt{r^{2}\log(n)\over n}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}p\log(n)\over n}+\epsilon^{2}\right)\leq c^{\prime\prime}\delta^{2}

(5.3)

holds. Then there exists a permutation
\pi:[r]\to[r]
such that with probability at least
1-re^{-2r}-r^{2}\delta-n^{-1}
,
\mathbf{R}\widehat{Q}_{\pi(k)}^{(0)}
satisfies

\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
for all
k\in[r]
with

\mu={1\over\sqrt{r}},\qquad\nu={1\over 3\sqrt{e}}{\delta\over\sqrt{r}}.

(5.4)

Consequently, conclusions in
Theorem

4
and
Theorem

5
hold.

Proof.

The proof appears in
Section

B.8.1
.
∎

For the random initialization in (
5.2
), the matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{(-k)}
ensures that
\widehat{Q}_{k+1}^{(0)}
is not close to any of the previously recovered solutions. This is crucial to establish the expression of
\mu
in
\mathcal{E}_{{\rm init}}^{(k)}
for
all

k\geq 2
.
Regarding the expression of
\nu
, it originates from the anti-concentration of the standard normal entries in
g_{(-k)}
. The union bounds argument over
k^{\prime}\in[r]\setminus\{k\}
and
k\in[r]
of such anti-concentration inequality leads to the term
r^{2}\delta
in the tail probability of
Theorem

3
.
If one adopts the multiple random initialization scheme discussed in
Remark

7
below, it is possible to improve
r^{2}\delta
to
\delta
but with the price of employing an additional clustering step.

Condition (
5.3
) corresponds to (
4.12
) with
\mu
and
\nu
specified in (
5.4
). It requires the lower dimension
r
to be much smaller than the sample size
n
, namely,
r^{\alpha}\ll n
for some
\alpha\in[5,13]
depending on the choice of
\delta
. It is worth mentioning that (
5.3
) is only needed for the worst case when the initialization gets close to satisfy (
4.10
), and mainly for a few initial iterates due to the contraction property in (
4.15
). As mentioned in
[
3
]
, the random initialization scheme works very well empirically for learning ICA model via tensor decomposition and deflation power iterations, but its analysis is still an open problem even under the ICA model. To the best of our knowledge,
Theorem

7
provides the first type of theoretical guarantees for using random initialization scheme in our general context.

Remark 7

(Multiple random initializations)
.

Instead of using one realization of
g_{(-k)}
, one could draw
g_{(-k)}
multiple times and compute the corresponding initializations as in (
5.2
). This will lead to many candidate solutions at each round
k\in[r]
. After all
r
rounds, a final clustering step is needed to select
r
among all obtained candidates as the final estimates. This idea has been proposed in the context of tensor decomposition
[
3
]
. Though such procedure could improve the tail probability in
Theorem

7
from
r^{2}\delta
to
\delta
, thereby relaxing (
5.3
), empirically we do not observe any evident benefit comparing to using a single random initialization.

5.2
Method of Moments based Initialization

We discuss in this subsection another initialization scheme that is based on a Method of Moments estimator of
\mathbf{A}
. The empirical moments we use are once again based on randomly sampled standard normal variables. To explain its main idea, we start by stating a population-level quantity from which one can identify one column of
\mathbf{A}
.

Let

\mathbf{G}
be a random
r\times r
matrix containing i.i.d. standard normal variables. Consider the following
r\times r
matrix

\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}A_{i}^{\top}\left(\kappa A_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{G}A_{i}\right).

Due to the orthogonality of columns in
\mathbf{A}
, we have two key observations.

(1)

If the two largest singular values of
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G})
have non-zero gap, then its leading left singular vector identifies one column of
\mathbf{A}
up to sign.

(2)

The singular values of
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G})
must take values from the set
\{\kappa|A_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G}A_{1}|,\ldots,\kappa|A_{r}^{\top}\mathbf{G}A_{r}|\}
.

The matrix
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G})
can thus be treated as a population level moment from which we can identify one column of
\mathbf{A}
. However, when
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G})
gets replaced by its sample estimate, the latter may not have large enough singular value gap to ensure consistency of its leading left singular vector. To address this issue,
[
3
,
4
]
in the context of learning overcomplete latent models via tensor methods and

[
7
]
under the ICA model, consider a procedure called multiple random slicings which is based on computing
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G})
multiple times from independent copies of
\mathbf{G}
.

Concretely, for some prespecified integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{G}_{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{G}_{(N)}
be independent copies of
\mathbf{G}
. For simplicity, denote

W_{ij}:=A_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{(i)}A_{j},\quad\text{for all }i\in[N],j\in[r].

By the orthogonality of columns in
\mathbf{A}
, one can verify that
\{W_{ij}\}_{i\in[N],j\in[r]}
are independent standard normal variables (see, the proof of
Lemma

13
). Therefore, for any given
j\in[r]
, concentration inequalities of the absolute value of the maximum of
N
independent standard normal variables (see
Lemma

11
) gives
that for all
\delta\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{i\in[N]}|W_{ij}|\geq\sqrt{2\log(N)}-{\log\log(N)+c\over 2\sqrt{2\log(N)}}-\sqrt{2\log(1/\delta)}\right\}\geq 1-\delta.

On the other hand, let
\bar{i}:=\bar{i}(j):=\argmax_{i\in[N]}|W_{ij}|
. Since
\bar{i}
is only defined through
\{W_{1j},\ldots,W_{Nj}\}
, it is independent of
W_{i\ell}
for all
i\in[N]
and
\ell\neq j
. This implies that
\{W_{\bar{i}\ell}\}_{\ell\in[r]\setminus\{j\}}
are
(r-1)
independent standard normal variables, and using
Lemma

11
again yields that for all
\delta\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{\ell\in[r]\setminus\{j\}}|W_{\bar{i}\ell}|~\leq\sqrt{2\log(2r)}+\sqrt{2\log(1/\delta)}\right\}\geq 1-\delta.

Fix any
\delta\geq 0
.
If we choose
N\geq 4r^{2}/\delta^{2}
, combing the previous two displays together with the observation (2) ensures that with probability at least
1-\delta/r
,

\sigma_{1}\bigl(\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(\bar{i})})\bigr)-\sigma_{2}\bigl(\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(\bar{i})})\bigr)=|W_{\bar{i}j}|-\max_{\ell\in[r]\setminus\{j\}}|W_{\bar{i}\ell}|~\geq~c^{\prime}\sqrt{\log(N)}.

Since
j\in[r]
in the definition of
\bar{i}
is chosen arbitrarily, we can choose the index

i_{*}:=\argmax_{i\in[N]}\left[\sigma_{1}\left(\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})\right)-\sigma_{2}\left(\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})\right)\right]

such that its corresponding matrix
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i_{*})})
has a gap between its largest two singular values no smaller than
c^{\prime}\sqrt{\log(N)}
. Thus, by observation (1), the left leading singular vector of
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i_{*})})
could be used to estimate one column of
\mathbf{A}
if the index
i_{*}
were known.

However, in practice we need to find both a suitable estimator of the index
i_{*}
and an estimator of
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i_{*})})
, which turns out to be a quite challenging task. In
[
3
,
4
]
the authors did not consider to estimate such
i_{*}
but instead employed an additional clustering step in the end to screen and group by all solutions obtained from multiple random slicings (see, also,
Remark

7
).
In the following we describe a simple procedure that meets our goals, and does not require either computing more than
r
candidate solutions or any additional clustering step.

For each
i\in[N]
, let the sample level counterpart of
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})
be

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})={1\over 3n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}U_{t}U_{t}^{\top}\left(U_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{(i)}U_{t}\right)-\mathbf{G}_{(i)}-\mathbf{G}_{(i)}^{\top},

(5.5)

with its singular values
\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{(i)}\geq\widehat{\sigma}_{2}^{(i)}\geq\cdots\geq\widehat{\sigma}_{r}^{(i)}
and the left leading singular vector
\widehat{u}^{(i)}_{1}
. On the one hand, we show in
Lemma

13
that
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})
consistently estimates
\overline{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})
uniformly over
i\in[N]
. On the other hand, we also show in the proof of
Theorem

8
that the sample analogue of
i_{*}
, defined as

\widehat{i}_{*}=\argmax_{i\in[N]}\left(\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{(i)}-\widehat{\sigma}_{2}^{(i)}\right),

ensures that
\sigma_{1}^{(\widehat{i}_{*})}-\sigma_{2}^{(\widehat{i}_{*})}\geq c^{\prime\prime}\sqrt{\log(N)}
with high probability.
By the observation (1) above, we thus propose to use the left leading singular vector of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(\widehat{i}_{*})})
for initialization in (
2.5
), namely,

\widehat{Q}_{1}^{(0)}=\widehat{u}^{(\widehat{i}_{*})}_{1}.

Theorem

8
below shows that in fact
\widehat{Q}_{1}^{(0)}
is already a consistent estimator of one column of
\mathbf{A}
, and could be further used as initialization to obtain faster rates.

For solving (
2.3
) the
k
th round with each
2\leq k\leq r
, we can repeat the whole procedure above except for using

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{(k)}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})=\widehat{\mathbf{P}}_{k-1}^{\perp}\widehat{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})\widehat{\mathbf{P}}_{k-1}^{\perp}

(5.6)

in place of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{G}_{(i)})
. Here the matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{P}}_{k-1}^{\perp}
given by (
5.1
) prevents from estimating duplicate columns in
\mathbf{A}
. More concretely, for each
i\in[N]
, let
\widehat{\sigma}^{(i,k)}_{1}\geq\widehat{\sigma}^{(i,k)}_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\widehat{\sigma}^{(i,k)}_{r}
and
\widehat{u}_{1}^{(i,k)}
be the singular values and the leading left singular vector, respectively, of (
5.6
). With

\widehat{i}_{*}:=\widehat{i}_{*}(k):=\argmax_{i\in[N]}\left(\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{(i,k)}-\widehat{\sigma}_{2}^{(i,k)}\right),

(5.7)

we propose to initialize (
2.5
) by

\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(0)}=\widehat{u}^{(\widehat{i}_{*},k)}_{1}.

(5.8)

In the following theorem we show that the above method of moments based initialization scheme provably ensures
\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{{\rm init}}
for all
k\in[r]
, and the established orders of
\mu
and
\nu
improve upon those for the random initialization. Recall
\omega_{n}
from (
4.2
).

Theorem 8

(Method of Moments based Initialization)
.

Grant Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
as well as
\kappa\geq c
for some absolute constant
c>0
. Choose
\gamma\leq c^{\prime}/\kappa
for some sufficiently small constant
c^{\prime}>0
. Further assume

r\left(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2}\right)\leq c^{\prime\prime}

(5.9)

for some small constant
c^{\prime\prime}>0
. For any
\delta\geq 0
, there exists a permutation
\pi:[r]\to[r]
such that with probability at least
1-n^{-C}-\delta
, the initialization
\widehat{Q}_{\pi(k)}^{(0)}
in (
5.8
) with
N\geq 4r^{2}/\delta^{2}
satisfies

\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{{\rm init}}(\mu,\nu)
for all
k\in[r]

with

\mu=1-C^{\prime}~r\left(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2}\right),\qquad\nu=\mu-\sqrt{1-\mu^{2}}.

(5.10)

Consequently, conclusions in
Theorem

4
and
Theorem

5
hold.

Proof.

The proof appears in
Section

B.9
.
∎

Note that condition (
5.9
) ensures
\mu\asymp\nu\asymp 1
.
Comparing to the random initialization in
Theorem

7
, due to the improved orders of
\mu
and
\nu
, condition (
5.9
) is weaker than (
5.3
) in terms of dependence on the lower dimension
r
. Condition (
5.9
) could also be relaxed to
(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2})\sqrt{r}\leq c^{\prime\prime}
by using a data splitting technique in
[
7
]
. However, since splitting the data potentially reduces finite sample performance and the lower dimension
r
in our context is typically small or moderate, we did not opt for such data splitting strategy.

Remark 8

(Rates of the method of moments estimator)
.

As a byproduct, our proof of
Theorem

8
also gives the convergence rate of the estimation error of the method of moments estimator. Specifically, let
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{MoM}=(\widehat{Q}_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,\widehat{Q}_{r}^{(0)})
with
\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(0)}
given by (
5.8
). We prove that, up to a signed permutation matrix,

\|\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{MoM}-\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\|_{F}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left(\omega_{n}+\epsilon^{2}\right)r^{3/2}\right).

Comparing to the estimator in
Theorem

4
, this rate is slower by a factor of
r
.

6
Improvement in the setting of structured noise

In this section, we offer two steps of improvement over the procedure described in
Section

2
when the covariance matrix of the additive noise
E
under model (
1.1
) is structured. We will focus on the case when
E
satisfies
3
with
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
, as this is an important case in various applications
[
43
]
.
Extension to other structures is discussed in
Remark

11
.
The first step of improvement advocates a different low-dimensional representation from the principal components, while the second step modifies the PGD iterate in (
2.5
) when performing deflation varimax rotation. Essentially these two improvements aim to reduce
\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}
and
\sigma_{N}^{2}
in
Theorem

2
, respectively.

6.1
Improvement over using the principal components

Recall the eigen-decomposition of
n^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}
in (
2.1
) and the SVD of
\mathbf{\Lambda}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}
in (
4.1
). Our proof of
Theorem

1
reveals that the bias of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
for approximating
\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Z}/\sigma
comes from using the eigenvalues
\mathbf{D}_{(r)}=\diag(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r})
to estimate
\sigma^{2}\mathbf{S}^{2}
. To reduce such bias, we propose to use

\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{(r)}=\widehat{\mathbf{D}}_{(r)}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}_{(r)}^{\top}\mathbf{X}

(6.1)

in place of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
, where

\widehat{\mathbf{D}}_{(r)}=\mathbf{D}_{(r)}-\mathbf{V}_{(r)}^{\top}\left(\widehat{\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}}~\mathbf{I}_{p}\right)\mathbf{V}_{(r)}=\mathbf{D}_{(r)}-\ \widehat{\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}}~\mathbf{I}_{r}

(6.2)

is a better estimator of
\sigma^{2}\mathbf{S}^{2}
than
\mathbf{D}_{(r)}
with

\widehat{\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}}={1\over p-r}\sum_{j>r}^{p}d_{j}.

(6.3)

The quantity
\widehat{\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{I}_{p}
can be treated as an estimator of
{\rm Cov}(E)=\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p}
. Indeed, we show in
Section

C.4
that
|\widehat{\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}}-\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\omega_{n})
.

The following theorem states a decomposition of
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{(r)}
similar to that of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
in
Theorem

1
but with a smaller approximation error.

Theorem 9
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, assume
r\log(n)\leq cn
and
\epsilon^{2}p\log(n)\leq cn
for some sufficiently small constant
c>0
and
\epsilon^{2}\leq 1
. Then there exists some
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{O}_{r\times r}
such that, with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
, one has

\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{(r)}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Z}/\sigma+\mathbf{N}+\mathbf{\Omega}\right)

(6.4)

where
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{\top}\mathbf{E}/\sigma
and
\mathbf{\Omega}=\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathbf{Z}/\sigma+\mathbf{N})

with

\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}\lesssim\omega_{n}.

Proof.

Its proof appears in
Section

C.4
.
∎

Comparing to
Theorem

1
, the bound of the approximation error
\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}
in
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{(r)}
is smaller due to using
\widehat{\mathbf{D}}_{(r)}
. From
Theorem

2
, a smaller
\|\mathbf{\Delta}\|_{\rm op}
also reduces the estimation error of the subsequent deflation varimax. Deploying
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{(r)}
in (
6.1
) thus can be treated as the first step of improvement, after which one could continue solving (
2.3
) by using
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{(r)}
in lieu of
\mathbf{U}_{(r)}
, and estimating the loading matrix as in (
2.7
) with
\mathbf{D}_{(r)}
replaced by
\widehat{\mathbf{D}}_{(r)}
.
In the following, we discuss a second step of improvement in solving (
2.3
) for performing deflation varimax.

6.2
Improvement of the deflation varimax rotation

Recall from
Theorem

2
that the addtive error matrix
\mathbf{N}
affects the error of estimating
\mathbf{A}
through
\sigma_{N}^{2}=\sup_{q\in\mathbb{S}^{r}}\|\mathbf{P}_{q}^{\perp}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}q\|_{2}
. In the discussion after
Theorem

2
we mentioned that
\sigma_{N}^{2}=0
when
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
is proportional to the identity matrix. In this section we show that as long as
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
can be estimated consistently, we can remove
\sigma_{N}^{2}
in
Theorem

2
by the proposed modification.

To explain the main idea, our proof of
Theorem

2
reveals that
\sigma_{N}^{2}
originates from one term in the difference between the Riemannian gradient,
\grad F(q;\mathbf{R}\mathbf{U}_{(r)})
, and its population-level counterpart (see,
Lemma

2
, for details). This term has an explicit expression and in fact can be viewed as the bias of estimating
\mathbf{A}
caused by the additive noise
\mathbf{N}
in (
4.3
). Therefore, the idea is to subtract this term by using its estimate during PGD.

Concretely, let
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{N}
be an estimator of
\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}\mathbf{R}
with
\mathbf{R}
defined in (
4.3
). For solving (
2.3
) the
k
th time with each
k\in[r]
, we propose to iterate as follows until convergence:

\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(\ell+1)}=P_{\mathbb{S}^{r}}\left(\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(\ell)}-\gamma~\widehat{\grad}F\bigl(\widehat{Q}_{k}^{(\ell)};\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\bigr)\right),\quad\text{for }\ \ell=0,1,2,\ldots

(6.5)

where for any
q\in\mathbb{S}^{r}
,

\widehat{\grad}F\bigl(q;\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\bigr):=\grad F\bigl(q;\mathbf{U}_{(r)}\bigr)+\left(1+q^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{N}q\right)\mathbf{P}_{q}^{\perp}\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{N}q,

(6.6)

Comparing to the PGD in (
2.5
), the second term in (
6.6
) is the estimator of the bias term mentioned above. Regarding initialization in (
6.5
), we can adopt the same initialization schemes discussed in
Section

5
.

Regarding the estimation of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}
, using its structure in (
4.6
) together with
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{E}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
gives
